<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Assuming the continuum hypothesis, we construct an abstract elementary class with Löwenheim–Skolem number ℵ<sub>0</sub> that is categorical in every sufficiently large cardinal but has at least two nonisomorphic models of cardinality $`\beth_{\omega_2}`$. Thus categoricity does not transfer down to the proposed bound $`\beth_{(2^{\aleph_0})^+}`$, which equals $`\beth_{\omega_2}`$ under CH. Consequently, if ZFC is consistent, the prescribed-threshold form of Shelah's categoricity conjecture is not provable in ZFC.

- **claim-ch-counterexample** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Assume the continuum hypothesis. There is an abstract elementary class $K$ in a countable finitary relational language such that* 1. *$\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)=\aleph_0$;* 2. *$K$ has at least two nonisomorphic models of cardinality $\beth_{\omega_2}=H(K)$;* 3. *putting $\Lambda=\beth_{(2^{\aleph_1})^+}$, the class $K$ is categorical in every cardinal $\mu\ge\Lambda$.* *In particular, $\lambda=\Lambda^+>H(K)$ is a categoricity cardinal from which downward transfer to $H(K)$ fails.
  - Explanation: Under the continuum hypothesis there is an abstract elementary class with countable Löwenheim–Skolem number that has two nonisomorphic models at the prescribed endpoint yet is categorical on a higher tail, so downward transfer to the endpoint fails.
- **claim-diagram-coding** (construction, [source](#stmt-col-coding)): There are injections $$\iota_k:\mathfrak D_k\longrightarrow S \qquad(k\ge1)$$ whose ranges are pairwise disjoint.
  - Explanation: Diagrams of every arity can be coded injectively by colors, with pairwise disjoint ranges for different arities.
- **claim-quotient-maps** (theorem, [source](#stmt-col-quotients)): Suppose the test in Definition <a href="#col:test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="col:test">4</a> passes. If $\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]$, then the label map $\ell_J$ induces an injective map $$Q_{\mathcal D_J}\longrightarrow Q_{\mathcal D_{[k]}}.$$ This map preserves tags and preserves and reflects $R$. The quotient maps for nested subsequences compose.
  - Explanation: When the subsequence test passes, restriction of labels induces injective maps between quotient diagrams that preserve tags and preserve and reflect the relation, and nested maps compose.
- **claim-base-order-size** (bound, [source](#stmt-aec-order-size)): Every order $B$ in a basic structure has cardinality at most $\aleph_1$.
  - Explanation: Local countability of bounded intervals bounds the base order by the first uncountable cardinal.
- **claim-structure-size** (bound, [source](#stmt-aec-size)): For every basic structure $M$, $$\label{aec:size-equation} \\|M\\|=\max\bigl(\|I^M\|,\|B^M\|,\aleph_0\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: The size of a basic structure is the largest of its index sort, base sort and the countable infinite cardinal.
- **claim-representative-independence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-aec-representatives)): The color in <a href="#aec:color" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aec:color">[aec:color]</a> is independent of $w$. If $\mathbf z,\mathbf z'$ are two row-choice systems, then, for each fixed $t$, their colors $c_t^{\mathbf z}(b)$ and $c_t^{\mathbf z'}(b)$ agree outside a countable subset of $B$. The full subsequence test for a fixed tuple therefore has the same truth value for the two systems outside a countable set.* *More strongly, suppose $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$. For a tuple in $\mathcal T(I^{M_0})$ and a point $b\in B^{M_1}\setminus B^{M_0}$, compute colors in $M_1$ using row representatives from $M_0$. These colors are independent of every such row choice, with no exceptional coordinates.
  - Explanation: Colors do not depend on the column representative, depend on row choices only outside a countable set, and are fully canonical at new base points of a basic inclusion.
- **claim-free-bit-arrays** (construction, [source](#stmt-aec-arrays)): Let $I\ne\varnothing$, and let $B$ be an order satisfying Definition <a href="#aec:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:basic">7</a>(a). For every family of binary sequences $(a_{t,b}\in 2^\omega:t\in\mathcal T(I),\,b\in B)$, there is a basic structure whose index and base sorts are disjoint copies of $I$ and $B$, with row-choice colors, under these identifications, $$c_t(b)=[a_{t,b}]_{=^*}.$$ If $B$ is countable, every basic structure with base sort $B$ is an object.
  - Explanation: Any prescribed family of bit sequences is realized by a basic structure, and a countable base automatically gives an object.
- **claim-order-and-coherence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-aec-order-coherence)): The class and strong relation are isomorphism invariant, $\le_K$ is a partial order whose comparisons are induced substructure inclusions, and coherence holds.
  - Explanation: The class and the strong relation are isomorphism invariant, the strong relation is a partial order of induced substructures, and coherence holds.
- **claim-directed-unions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-aec-directed)): Let $(M_i:i\in D)$ be a nonempty set-indexed family of objects directed under strong inclusion: any two members have a common strong upper bound within the family. Its union $U$ is an object, and $M_i\le_K U$ for every $i\in D$. If $N$ is an object with $M_i\le_K N$ for all $i\in D$, then $U\le_K N$.
  - Explanation: Directed unions of objects under strong inclusion are objects, each member is strong in the union, and the union is strong in any common strong upper bound.
- **claim-convex-hull** (bound, [source](#stmt-aec-convex-hull)): Let $B$ be a nonempty dense linear order without endpoints, with countable bounded intervals. For every infinite cardinal $\kappa$ and every $D\subseteq B$ of size at most $\kappa$, there is a nonempty convex suborder $B_0$ containing $D$, of size at most $\kappa$, which is dense and has no endpoints.
  - Explanation: A small subset of the base order sits inside a dense convex suborder without endpoints of no larger size.
- **claim-downward-ls** (theorem, [source](#stmt-aec-ls)): For every $M\in\mathcal C$ and every $A\subseteq \|M\|$, there is $N\le_K M$ such that $A\subseteq \|N\|$ and $\\|N\\|\le \|A\|+\aleph_0$.
  - Explanation: Every requested subset lies inside a strong submodel of size at most the subset plus the countable infinite cardinal.
- **claim-class-is-aec** (theorem, [source](#stmt-aec-class)): The pair $K=(\mathcal C,\le_K)$ of Definition <a href="#aec:objects" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:objects">12</a> is an abstract elementary class in a fixed countable finitary relational language, with $$\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)=\aleph_0.$$ It has the union and common-upper-bound properties for every nonempty set-indexed directed family of strong substructures.
  - Explanation: The class of objects with strong inclusion is an abstract elementary class in a countable finitary relational language with countable Löwenheim–Skolem number.
- **claim-domination-functions** (construction, [source](#stmt-end-domination)): There are functions $f_\alpha:\delta\longrightarrow\delta$, for $\alpha<\eta$, such that for all $\gamma<\alpha<\eta$ the set $$E_{\gamma,\alpha} =\{\beta<\delta:f_\gamma(\beta)\geq f_\alpha(\beta)\}$$ is countable.
  - Explanation: A sequence of functions on the first uncountable cardinal, increasing modulo countable sets, is built by transfinite recursion.
- **claim-hierarchy-size** (bound, [source](#stmt-end-hierarchy-size)): The cumulative hierarchy satisfies $$\|V_{\omega+\gamma}\|=\beth_\gamma \quad\text{for every ordinal }\gamma.$$ In particular, $\|V_\eta\|=\beth_\eta$, and every element of $V_\eta$ has rank below $\eta$.
  - Explanation: Stages of the cumulative hierarchy beyond the first infinite stage have beth cardinalities.
- **claim-countable-closures** (construction, [source](#stmt-end-countable-closures)): There is a family $$(D_X:\varnothing\ne X\in[D]^{<\omega})$$ of nonempty countable subsets of $D$ such that:* 1. *$X\subseteq D_X$;* 2. *$D_Y\subseteq D_X$ whenever $\varnothing\ne Y\subseteq X$;* 3. *the relation of actual membership on $D_X$ is extensional: for distinct $a,b\in D_X$, some $w\in D_X$ belongs to exactly one of $a,b$.* *We may also fix surjections $d_X:\omega\longrightarrow D_X$ such that $d_{\{x\}}(0)=x$ for each $x\in D$.
  - Explanation: Each nonempty finite set of the universe gets a countable extensional closure, monotone in the set, with fixed enumerations.
- **claim-endpoint-diagrams** (theorem, [source](#stmt-end-diagrams)): For every tuple $t$ as above, the equality quotient of $\mathcal D_{t,\beta}$ identifies with $D_X$, with its actual membership relation, for every $\beta<\delta$. The diagrams satisfy the following additional properties.* 1. *If $\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]$, restriction of $\mathcal D_{t,\beta}$ to the labels belonging to $J$, with the increasing reindexing of positions, is exactly $\mathcal D_{t\restriction J,\beta}$.* 2. *If $\beta\notin F_X$, then every diagram $\mathcal D_{t\restriction J,\beta}$, including the full diagram, is valid.* *Consequently, for every $\beta\notin F_X$, the colors $$s_{t\restriction J,\beta} =\iota_{\|J\|}(\mathcal D_{t\restriction J,\beta}), \qquad \varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k],$$ pass the full subsequence test of Definition <a href="#col:test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="col:test">4</a>.
  - Explanation: The tuple diagrams have the closure as quotient, are compatible with restriction, and are valid outside a countable exceptional set, so the subsequence test passes there.
- **claim-two-endpoint-models** (theorem, [source](#stmt-end-two-models)): The class $K$ has two nonisomorphic models of cardinality $\beth_\eta$. One has an order sort of cardinality $\aleph_1$, and the other has a countable order sort.
  - Explanation: One model with uncountable base order and one with countable base order, both of the endpoint size, cannot be isomorphic.
- **claim-tagged-collapse** (theorem, [source](#stmt-col-collapse)): Let $Q$ be a set with an extensional binary relation $R$ and a function $\rho:Q\to\delta$ such that $$yRx\ \Longrightarrow\ \rho(y)<\rho(x).$$ Then there is an injection $F:Q\to V_\delta$ satisfying $$F(x)=\{F(y):yRx\}.$$ More precisely, $F(x)\in V_{\rho(x)+1}$ for every $x\in Q$.
  - Explanation: An extensional relation whose tags strictly increase along the relation collapses injectively into the cumulative hierarchy below the tag bound.
- **claim-no-fixed-pattern** (theorem, [source](#stmt-col-no-pattern)): There is no sequence $(s_j:j\ge1)$ of elements of $S$ such that $$\label{col:fixed-pattern} T_k\bigl((s_{\|J\|})_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]}\bigr) \quad\text{holds for every }k\ge1.$$
  - Explanation: No single sequence of colors can pass the full subsequence test at every finite arity, since a direct limit would inject an overly large set into the cumulative hierarchy.
- **claim-lambda-cardinal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cat-cardinals)): The cardinal $\Lambda$ is a strong limit and $$\mathop{\mathrm{cf}}(\Lambda)=\xi^+>\xi.$$ If $\zeta<\Lambda$ is infinite, then both $\xi^\zeta$ and $(\xi^\zeta)^+$ are below $\Lambda$.
  - Explanation: The tail threshold is a strong limit whose cofinality exceeds the continuum-power parameter, and powers below it stay below it.
- **claim-finite-partition** (bound, [source](#stmt-cat-finite-partition)): For every positive integer $m$ and every infinite cardinal $\chi<\Lambda$, there is a cardinal $\mu<\Lambda$ such that every map $c:[\mu]^m\to C$ with $\|C\|\le\xi$ has a homogeneous subset of cardinality $\chi$.
  - Explanation: For every finite arity and every target size below the tail threshold, a source cardinal below it forces homogeneous sets of that size.
- **claim-finite-patterns** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cat-finite-patterns)): Let $X$ be a set with $\|X\|\ge\Lambda$. For each positive integer $j$, let $c_j:[X]^j\to C_j$, where $\|C_j\|\le\xi$. There are colors $p_j\in C_j$ such that for every positive integer $k$ there is $A_k\in[X]^k$ satisfying $$c_j(A)=p_j\qquad (1\le j\le k,\ A\in[A_k]^j).$$ The sets $A_k$ need not be nested.
  - Explanation: Colors can be chosen so that every finite size has a witnessing set carrying exactly those colors, without nested witnesses or an infinite homogeneous set.
- **claim-uncountable-base-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-cat-bounded)): If $M\in K$ and $B^M$ is uncountable, then $\|I^M\|<\Lambda$.
  - Explanation: A model with uncountable base order has index sort strictly below the tail threshold; otherwise the forbidden fixed pattern would arise.
- **claim-countable-base-iso** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cat-countable-base)): If $M,N\in K$ have countable base orders and $\|I^M\|=\|I^N\|$, then $M\cong N$.
  - Explanation: Models with countable base orders and equal index sort sizes are isomorphic, by cancelling difference matrices with finite row and column translations.
- **claim-tail-categoricity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cat-tail)): The class $K$ is categorical in every cardinal $\mu\ge\Lambda$.
  - Explanation: The class is categorical at every cardinal from the tail threshold on, including the threshold itself.
- **claim-zfc-nonprovability** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-cor-consistency)): ZFC proves $\mathrm{CH}\Rightarrow\Theta$. For each fixed $\Phi$ as above, it also proves $\mathrm{CH}\Rightarrow\neg\Phi$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{Con}}(\mathrm{ZFC}) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathop{\mathrm{Con}}(\mathrm{ZFC}+\Theta) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathop{\mathrm{Con}}(\mathrm{ZFC}+\neg\Phi).$$ If ZFC is consistent, no such $\Phi$ is a theorem of ZFC.
  - Explanation: Combined with relative consistency of the continuum hypothesis, no fixed first-order formulation of the prescribed-threshold statement is provable in ZFC if ZFC is consistent.
- **claim-no-amalgamation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-no-amalgamation)): The class $K$ has neither joint embedding nor amalgamation. The failure of amalgamation can be witnessed over a countable model.
  - Explanation: The class lacks joint embedding and amalgamation, so structural transfer theorems requiring them do not apply.
- **claim-countable-extension** (construction, [source](#stmt-bool-extension)): Every countable $M\in K$ has a countable strong extension $M'$ with $I^{M'}=I^M$ and $B^M\subsetneq B^{M'}$.
  - Explanation: Every countable model has a countable strong extension that adds base points and keeps the index sort.
- **claim-canonical-point-obstruction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-bool-obstruction)): If $H\in K$ has countable base, its canonical point $p_H$ does not factor through $\operatorname{Sh}(\mathcal C_0^{\mathrm{op}},J_{\neg\neg})$. This applies, in particular, to every model on the categoricity tail $\\|H\\|\ge\Lambda$.
  - Explanation: For a model with countable base order, the canonical point does not factor through the double-negation subtopos, because a dense cosieve is not lifted by the model.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **arity-diagram** ([source](#stmt-col-diagram)): An *arity-$k$ diagram* is a triple $\mathcal D=(E,R,\rho)$, where $E,R$ are binary relations on $\mathcal L_k$ and $\rho:\mathcal L_k\to\delta$. It is *valid* if the following conditions hold. 1. $E$ is an equivalence relation. The relation $R$ and the tags respect $E$: whenever $aEa'$ and $bEb'$, $$aRb\ \Longleftrightarrow\ a'Rb', \qquad \rho(a)=\rho(a').$$ 2. On the quotient $Q_{\mathcal D}=\mathcal L_k/E$, use $R$ and $\rho$ also for the induced relation and tag function. They satisfy $$yRx\ \Longrightarrow\ \rho(y)<\rho(x).$$ The relation $R$ is extensional: distinct $x,x'\in Q_{\mathcal D}$ have different predecessor sets $$\{y\in Q_{\mathcal D}:yRx\} \quad\hbox{and}\quad \{y\in Q_{\mathcal D}:yRx'\}.$$ 3. The labels $(\{j\},0)$, for $j\in[k]$, lie in pairwise distinct $E$-classes.
- **full-subsequence-test** ([source](#stmt-col-test)): Given $k\ge1$ and colors $(s_J:\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k])$, let $$T_k\bigl((s_J)_J\bigr)$$ mean that both of the following conditions hold. 1. For every nonempty $J\subseteq[k]$, the color $s_J$ decodes under $\iota_{\|J\|}$ to a valid diagram $\mathcal D_J$. 2. For every nonempty $J\subseteq[k]$, $$\mathcal D_J =\mathcal D_{[k]}\restriction J.$$ For an ordered tuple $t=(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ of distinct elements, write $t\restriction J=(x_{j_1},\ldots,x_{j_m})$. Colors attached to $t$ and its nonempty subsequences pass the test when $T_k((s_{t\restriction J})_J)$ holds.
- **abstract-elementary-class** ([source](#stmt-aec-definition)): An *abstract elementary class* is a pair $K=(\mathcal C,\le_K)$, where $\mathcal C$ is a class of set-sized structures in one fixed set-sized finitary language $\tau$, and $\le_K$ is a partial order on $\mathcal C$, with the following properties. Write $\|M\|$ for a structure’s underlying set and $\\|M\\|$ for its cardinality. 1. *Isomorphisms and substructures.* The class is closed under $\tau$-isomorphisms; $M\le_K N$ implies that $M$ is a $\tau$-substructure of $N$; and if $f:N\cong N'$ and $M\le_K N$, then $f[M]\le_K N'$. 2. *Coherence.* If $M_0$ is a $\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and $M_0\le_K M_2$ and $M_1\le_K M_2$, then $M_0\le_K M_1$. 3. *Unions and smoothness.* For every nonzero ordinal $\gamma$ and every increasing chain $(M_i:i<\gamma)$ under $\le_K$, its union $U$ belongs to $\mathcal C$, and $M_i\le_K U$ for all $i<\gamma$. If also $M_i\le_K N$ for every $i<\gamma$, where $N\in\mathcal C$, then $U\le_K N$. 4. *Downward Löwenheim–Skolem property.* There is an infinite cardinal $\theta\ge\|\tau\|+\aleph_0$ such that, for every $M\in\mathcal C$ and every $A\subseteq \|M\|$, there exists $N\le_K M$ with $A\subseteq \|N\|$ and $\\|N\\|\le \|A\|+\theta$. The least such infinite cardinal $\theta$ is denoted by $\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)$.
- **basic-structure** ([source](#stmt-aec-basic)): A basic structure has the following data, with pairwise disjoint underlying carriers. 1. A nonempty set $I$, and a nonempty dense linear order $B$ without endpoints in which every bounded interval is countable. 2. A group sort $G(B)$ coding $[B]^{<\omega}$ under symmetric difference. More precisely, an incidence relation determines a bijection $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}:G(B)\longrightarrow[B]^{<\omega}, \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u+u')=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u)\triangle\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u').$$ There is also a group sort $G_*$ identified, by a separate unary singleton label for each of its elements, with $[\omega]^{<\omega}$ under symmetric difference. These labels exhaust $G_*$. 3. For each $t\in\mathcal T(I)$ and $n<\omega$, a nonempty set $Z_{t,n}$ with a free transitive action of $G(B)$. For each $t\in\mathcal T(I)$ and $b\in B$, a nonempty set $W_{t,b}$ with a free transitive action of $G_*$. We write both actions additively. Thus each element of a fiber is obtained uniquely by translating any one chosen element of that fiber. 4. A binary relation on row and column points, written as its indicator $e(z,w)\in\{0,1\}$. It can hold only when the row and column have the same tuple $t$. On these pairs it satisfies $$\label{aec:bit} e(z+u,w+v) = e(z,w)+\boldsymbol 1_{b\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u)} +\boldsymbol 1_{n\in v}\pmod 2$$ for $z\in Z_{t,n}$, $w\in W_{t,b}$, $u\in G(B)$, and $v\in G_*$. In the last term $v$ is read through its fixed finite-subset label. Here $\boldsymbol 1_P$ is $1$ when $P$ holds and $0$ otherwise. The domain consists exactly of the listed carriers. There are no additional relations or distinguished representatives.
- **basic-inclusion** ([source](#stmt-aec-basic-inclusion)): For basic structures $M_0,M_1$, write $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$ if $M_0$ is an induced $\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and the following further conditions hold: 1. $G_*^{M_0}=G_*^{M_1}$ as underlying sets; 2. $B^{M_0}$ is a convex suborder of $B^{M_1}$; 3. for every $u\in G(B)^{M_0}$, $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}^{M_1}(u)=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}^{M_0}(u)$. All inclusions here and below allow equality.
- **object-and-strong-substructure** ([source](#stmt-aec-objects)): A basic structure is an *object* if $E_t(\mathbf z)$ is countable for every $t\in\mathcal T(I)$, for one, equivalently every, row-choice system $\mathbf z$. Let $\mathcal C$ be the class of objects. For $M_0,M_1\in\mathcal C$, declare $M_0\le_K M_1$ if $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$, and, for every $t\in\mathcal T(I^{M_0})$ and every $b\in B^{M_1}\setminus B^{M_0}$, the full test for $t$ passes when its colors in $M_1$ are computed using row representatives in $M_0$, for $t$ and all its nonempty subsequences. Put $K=(\mathcal C,\le_K)$.
- **row-choice-system** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A *row-choice system* in a basic structure is a family $\mathbf z=(z_{t,n}:t\in\mathcal T(I),\,n<\omega)$ with $z_{t,n}\in Z_{t,n}$.
- **homogeneous-subset** ([source](#pm-h-20)): A subset $Y\subseteq X$ is *homogeneous* for a map $c:[X]^m\to C$ if $c$ is constant on $[Y]^m$.

      "interpretation": "A structure with an index sort, a locally countable dense base order, finite-support group sorts, torsor fibers and a bit relation.",

          "anchor": "stmt-aec-basic-inclusion",
          "exact_quote": "For basic structures $M_0,M_1$, write $M_0\\preccurlyeq_{\\mathrm b}M_1$ if $M_0$ is an induced $\\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and the following further conditions hold:\n\n1.  $G_*^{M_0}=G_*^{M_1}$ as underlying sets;\n\n2.  $B^{M_0}$ is a convex suborder of $B^{M_1}$;\n\n3.  for every $u\\in G(B)^{M_0}$, $\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}^{M_1}(u)=\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}^{M_0}(u)$.\n\nAll inclusions here and below allow equality.",

Let $\\mathcal C$ be the class of objects.\n\nFor $M_0,M_1\\in\\mathcal C$, declare $M_0\\le_K M_1$ if $M_0\\preccurlyeq_{\\mathrm b}M_1$, and, for every $t\\in\\mathcal T(I^{M_0})$ and every $b\\in B^{M_1}\\setminus B^{M_0}$, the full test for $t$ passes when its colors in $M_1$ are computed using row representatives in $M_0$, for $t$ and all its nonempty subsequences. Put $K=(\\mathcal C,\\le_K)$.",

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-hanf-threshold | Prescribed categoricity threshold of an abstract elementary class | = | $\beth_{(2^\kappa)^+}$ | kappa is an infinite cardinal |
| q-ls-number | Löwenheim–Skolem number of the constructed class | = | $\aleph_0$ | countable finitary relational language |
| q-endpoint | Endpoint cardinal with two nonisomorphic models | = | $\beth_{\omega_2}$ | equals the prescribed threshold of the constructed class |
| q-tail-threshold | Tail threshold above which the class is categorical | = | $\beth_{(2^{\aleph_1})^+}$ | categoricity holds at every cardinal at least this value |
| q-base-order-size | Cardinality of the base order | ≤ | $\aleph_1$ | every base order |
| q-structure-size | Cardinality of a basic structure | = | $\max\bigl(\|I^M\|,\|B^M\|,\aleph_0\bigr)$ | every basic structure |
| q-color-space-size | Cardinality of the color space of binary sequences modulo finite differences | = | $2^{\aleph_0}$ | the quotient set of binary sequences |
| q-hierarchy-stage-size | Cardinality of a stage of the cumulative hierarchy | = | $\beth_\gamma$ | stage indexed by the first infinite stage plus gamma |
| q-lambda-cofinality | Cofinality of the tail threshold | = | $\xi^+$ | tail threshold of the construction |
| q-profile-palette | Number of whole color profiles on a base order | ≤ | $\xi$ | base order of size at most the first uncountable cardinal |
| q-index-bound | Index sort of a model with uncountable base order | < | $\Lambda$ | models with uncountable base order |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-coding** [claim](#stmt-col-coding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-col-coding): Count classes of eventual equality and use the continuum hypothesis to bound diagram data. -> disjoint injective coding maps
- **step-quotients** [claim](#stmt-col-quotients) / [proof](#proof-stmt-col-quotients): Compare equivalence and relation of a subtuple diagram with those of the full diagram along label maps. -> injective quotient maps preserving tags
- **step-order-size** [claim](#stmt-aec-order-size) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-order-size): Cover final segments by countable bounded intervals using a recursive increasing sequence. -> bound on the base order
- **step-size** [claim](#stmt-aec-size) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-size): Count tuples, row fibers and column fibers. -> size formula
- **step-representatives** [claim](#stmt-aec-representatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-representatives): Use finite translators and countable unions of finite supports. -> canonical colors outside countable sets
- **step-arrays** [claim](#stmt-aec-arrays) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-arrays): Build regular torsors over tagged copies of the groups and set the bit relation by a parity formula. -> basic structure realizing the array
- **step-coherence** [claim](#stmt-aec-order-coherence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-order-coherence): Check invariance, reflexivity, transitivity and coherence by transporting colors. -> partial order with coherence
- **step-directed** [claim](#stmt-aec-directed) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-directed): Place finitely many elements in a common member and verify basic data, object condition and strongness. -> union is an object and an upper bound
- **step-convex-hull** [claim](#stmt-aec-convex-hull) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-convex-hull): Close a small set under neighbors and take its convex hull. -> small dense convex suborder
- **step-downward-ls** [claim](#stmt-aec-ls) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-ls): Collect indices, supports and bad sets, take a convex hull and restrict the structure. -> small strong submodel
- **step-class-is-aec** [claim](#stmt-aec-class) / [proof](#proof-stmt-aec-class): Assemble the axiom verifications. -> abstract elementary class
- **step-domination** [claim](#stmt-end-domination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-end-domination): Transfinite recursion with a supremum over countably many earlier functions. -> functions increasing modulo countable sets
- **step-hierarchy-size** [claim](#stmt-end-hierarchy-size) / [proof](#proof-stmt-end-hierarchy-size): Induction on the stage with successor and limit cases. -> stage cardinalities
- **step-countable-closures** [claim](#stmt-end-countable-closures) / [proof](#proof-stmt-end-countable-closures): Adjoin distinguishing witnesses iteratively, recursing on the size of the finite set. -> countable extensional closures
- **step-endpoint-diagrams** [claim](#stmt-end-diagrams) / [proof](#proof-stmt-end-diagrams): Define tags through the domination functions and show validity outside countable exceptional sets. -> diagrams passing the test off countable sets
- **step-two-models** [claim](#stmt-end-two-models) / [proof](#proof-stmt-end-two-models): Realize the endpoint colors with uncountable base and a trivial array with countable base, then compare base sorts. -> two nonisomorphic endpoint models
- **step-collapse** [claim](#stmt-col-collapse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-col-collapse): Recursion on tags with induction for the rank bound and injectivity. -> injection into the hierarchy
- **step-no-pattern** [claim](#stmt-col-no-pattern) / [proof](#proof-stmt-col-no-pattern): Form a direct limit of quotient diagrams over finite subsets of a large ordered set and collapse it. -> contradiction
- **step-cardinals** [claim](#stmt-cat-cardinals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-cardinals): Use continuity and strict increase of the beth sequence and regularity of a successor. -> strong limit with large cofinality
- **step-finite-partition** [claim](#stmt-cat-finite-partition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-finite-partition): Induction on arity using a residual-set tree and an unnamed point. -> homogeneous sets of prescribed size
- **step-finite-patterns** [claim](#stmt-cat-finite-patterns) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-finite-patterns): Choose colors recursively keeping an invariant of unbounded samples. -> fixed colors with separate witnesses
- **step-uncountable-base-bound** [claim](#stmt-cat-bounded) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-bounded): Extract a fixed pattern from a large index sort and pick a coordinate outside countably many bad sets. -> bound on index sort
- **step-countable-base-iso** [claim](#stmt-cat-countable-base) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-countable-base): Back-and-forth for the base order, then triangular finite translators to cancel the difference matrix. -> explicit isomorphism
- **step-tail-categoricity** [claim](#stmt-cat-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-tail): Show existence by a free array and uniqueness by forcing a countable base. -> categoricity on the tail
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cat-tail-2): Combine the class, the endpoint models and tail categoricity with Cantor's theorem. -> failure of downward transfer
- **step-consistency** [claim](#stmt-cor-consistency) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-consistency): Formalize the construction as a set-theoretic sentence and invoke relative consistency of the continuum hypothesis. -> nonprovability if ZFC is consistent
- **step-no-amalgamation** [claim](#stmt-prop-no-amalgamation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-amalgamation): Enlarge only the index sort of a countable submodel and contradict the index bound for a common extension. -> failure of joint embedding and amalgamation
- **step-countable-extension** [claim](#stmt-bool-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bool-extension): Build compatible colors, add a copy of the rationals to the base order and extend torsors and bits. -> countable strong extension adding base points
- **step-canonical-point** [claim](#stmt-bool-obstruction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-bool-obstruction): Exhibit a dense cosieve whose image under the canonical point misses a map. -> non-factorization

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-endpoint-failure**: Under the continuum hypothesis the constructed class has Löwenheim–Skolem number aleph-zero, two nonisomorphic models at the prescribed endpoint, and categoricity on a higher tail, so downward transfer to the endpoint fails.
- **inv-tail-categoricity**: The class is categorical in every cardinal at or above the tail threshold.
- **inv-uncountable-base-bound**: Every model with uncountable base order has index sort strictly below the tail threshold.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-downward-transfer**: Assume uniqueness of models on a high tail forces uniqueness at the prescribed lower threshold for arbitrary abstract elementary classes. -> The constructed class is categorical on the tail yet has two nonisomorphic models at the threshold.
- **anti-bare-aec-assumptions**: Apply amalgamation-based transfer theorems to the constructed class. -> The class has neither joint embedding nor amalgamation, so such theorems give no conclusion.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results are existential set-theoretic statements about models of transfinite cardinality under the continuum hypothesis; no finite procedure computes the class, its models, or the bounds, so no executable reference implementation exists.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The results concern infinite structures of cardinality beth-type and a set-theoretic hypothesis. No finite program, API or data format follows from them. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper is a dynamical, stochastic or rule-based system that could be simulated. The models are uncountable set-sized structures. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper's results are existence and nonexistence statements, not procedures that a tool could run. A proof-assistant formalisation would encode statements, not compute them. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The partition-tree and diagonal-recursion arguments run over transfinite index sets and unbounded cardinals. They are proof devices, not finite algorithms with a complexity. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The torsor fibers, finite-support groups and base orders are abstract model-theoretic sorts of infinite size. They do not specify a storable representation with operations and costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No finite circuit, memory hierarchy or parallel computation model appears. The cardinalities involved are far beyond any physical device. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no adversary, hardness assumption or security reduction. Uniqueness of models up to isomorphism is not a security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, data distribution or generalisation statement occurs. The colors are binary sequences modulo finite change, not features. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no objective function, feasible set or resource bound to optimise. The bounds on index sorts are cardinal inequalities in a model-theoretic class. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The failure of joint embedding and amalgamation concerns embeddings of infinite structures, not replicas, messages or consensus. No fault model is present. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The nearest external use is as a foundational comparison point for formal specifications in set theory and model theory. The paper does not supply a construction for it, and the nonprovability claim is conditional on consistency of ZFC. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-ch-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The counterexample is proved only in ZFC together with the continuum hypothesis. The cardinal computations for the endpoint, the color space and the tail threshold all use it. Blocked use: ['Reading the construction as a counterexample in ZFC without the continuum hypothesis', 'Reusing the specific cardinal values outside the continuum-hypothesis regime']
- **limit-conditional-nonprovability** (formal_scope): The logical conclusion is one-sided. If ZFC is consistent, the prescribed-threshold statement is not provable in ZFC, under each fixed first-order formulation. This is neither a ZFC proof of its negation nor a consistency result for the statement itself. Blocked use: ['Claiming the prescribed-threshold conjecture is refuted outright in ZFC', 'Claiming independence in both directions']
- **limit-bare-aec-regime** (counterexample_scope): The failure of downward transfer is for abstract elementary classes with no amalgamation, joint embedding, tameness or absence of maximal models assumed. It says nothing against transfer theorems that impose such structure. Blocked use: ['Using the result to dispute transfer theorems that assume amalgamation and tameness', 'Applying amalgamation-based transfer results to the constructed class']
- **limit-specific-class-tail** (counterexample_scope): The counterexample is one specific class. The categoricity cardinal above the endpoint is a tail point of that class, and categoricity there does not descend to the endpoint. Blocked use: ['Concluding that every abstract elementary class has two models at its prescribed threshold', 'Inferring failure of qualitative eventual categoricity from the endpoint failure']
- **limit-uncountable-base-index-bound** (edge_case): The index bound below the tail threshold applies only to models whose base order is uncountable. Models with countable base orders are unconstrained by it. Blocked use: ['Bounding the index sort of a countable-base model by the tail threshold', 'Using the bound outside the constructed class']
- **limit-canonical-point-scope** (formal_scope): The non-factorization result concerns the canonical point of a model with countable base and the full double-negation topology. It is an obstruction to one proof step, not a classification of all points of the Booleanization. Blocked use: ['Claiming the Booleanization has no points', 'Claiming every construction of points for the class fails']
- **limit-choice-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The coding maps, closure enumerations and recursion injections are chosen with the axiom of choice. No finite procedure computes the class or its models. Blocked use: ['Extracting an executable construction of the models', 'Treating the coding maps as explicitly defined']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 240 (`lean/docs/240.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A CH obstruction to a prescribed categoricity threshold](../../preprints/A-CH-Obstruction-to-a-Prescribed-Categoricity-Threshold-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Under the continuum hypothesis, the formalized result refutes the proposed transfer of categoricity down to the Hanf threshold. It gives an abstract elementary class in a countable relational language, with Löwenheim–Skolem number $\aleph_0$, that has two nonisomorphic models at $\beth_{\omega_2}=h(\aleph_0)$ but is categorical in every cardinal at least $\beth_{(2^{\aleph_1})^+}$.
>
> No amalgamation, joint embedding, tameness, or absence-of-maximal-models assumption is imposed. The later canonical-point obstruction and the separate eventual-categoricity theorem are not included.

Comparator source `CHObstruction.lean` (3654 bytes, sha256 `b58c11900fa0d3873614f086562b2d46acd5f8d47e67b7d79a1220a274ed9b3b`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

universe u

noncomputable section

namespace CHObstruction

open Cardinal Ordinal Order

structure RelModel (L : ℕ → Type) where
  carrier : ZFSet.{u}
  rel : (n : ℕ) → L n → (Fin n → carrier) → Prop

namespace RelModel

variable {L : ℕ → Type}

structure IsSubstructure (M N : RelModel.{u} L) : Prop where
  subset : M.carrier ⊆ N.carrier
  restrict : ∀ (n) (r : L n) (a : Fin n → M.carrier),
    M.rel n r a ↔ N.rel n r (fun i => ⟨(a i).val, subset (a i).property⟩)

structure Iso (M N : RelModel.{u} L) where
  toEquiv : M.carrier ≃ N.carrier
  map_rel : ∀ (n) (r : L n) (a : Fin n → M.carrier),
    M.rel n r a ↔ N.rel n r (toEquiv ∘ a)

def IsUnion {ι : Type u} (F : ι → RelModel.{u} L) (U : RelModel.{u} L) : Prop :=
  (∀ i, IsSubstructure (F i) U) ∧
  ∀ x : U.carrier, ∃ i, x.val ∈ (F i).carrier

end RelModel

structure ClassData (L : ℕ → Type) where
  objects : RelModel.{u} L → Prop
  strong : RelModel.{u} L → RelModel.{u} L → Prop

namespace ClassData

variable {L : ℕ → Type} (K : ClassData.{u} L)

def LSBound (θ : Cardinal.{u}) : Prop :=
  ℵ₀ ≤ θ ∧ Cardinal.lift.{u, 0} (# (Σ n, L n)) ≤ θ ∧
  ∀ M, K.objects M → ∀ A : ZFSet.{u}, A ⊆ M.carrier →
    ∃ N, K.objects N ∧ K.strong N M ∧ A ⊆ N.carrier ∧ N.carrier.card ≤ A.card + θ

def HasLSNumber (θ : Cardinal.{u}) : Prop :=
  K.LSBound θ ∧ ∀ θ', K.LSBound θ' → θ ≤ θ'

structure IsAEC : Prop where
  strong_objects : ∀ {M N}, K.strong M N → K.objects M ∧ K.objects N
  refl : ∀ {M}, K.objects M → K.strong M M
  antisymm : ∀ {M N}, K.strong M N → K.strong N M → M = N
  trans : ∀ {M N P}, K.strong M N → K.strong N P → K.strong M P
  substructure : ∀ {M N}, K.strong M N → M.IsSubstructure N
  iso_objects : ∀ {M N}, K.objects M → Nonempty (M.Iso N) → K.objects N
  iso_strong : ∀ {M N M' N'} (h : K.strong M N)
    (e : M.Iso M') (f : N.Iso N'),
    (∀ x : M.carrier,
      (e.toEquiv x).val =
        (f.toEquiv ⟨x.val, (substructure h).subset x.property⟩).val) →
    K.strong M' N'
  coherence : ∀ {M₀ M₁ M₂}, M₀.IsSubstructure M₁ →
    K.strong M₀ M₂ → K.strong M₁ M₂ → K.strong M₀ M₁
  chain_union : ∀ (γ : Ordinal.{u}), γ ≠ 0 →
    ∀ (F : γ.ToType → RelModel.{u} L),
    (∀ i, K.objects (F i)) →
    (∀ i j, i ≤ j → K.strong (F i) (F j)) →
    ∃ U, K.objects U ∧ RelModel.IsUnion F U ∧
      (∀ i, K.strong (F i) U) ∧
      (∀ N, K.objects N → (∀ i, K.strong (F i) N) → K.strong U N)
  downward : ∃ θ, K.LSBound θ

def Categorical (μ : Cardinal.{u}) : Prop :=
  (∃ M, K.objects M ∧ M.carrier.card = μ) ∧
  ∀ M N, K.objects M → K.objects N →
    M.carrier.card = μ → N.carrier.card = μ → Nonempty (M.Iso N)

def TwoModels (μ : Cardinal.{u}) : Prop :=
  ∃ M N, K.objects M ∧ K.objects N ∧
    M.carrier.card = μ ∧ N.carrier.card = μ ∧ ¬ Nonempty (M.Iso N)

end ClassData

def CH : Prop := (2 : Cardinal.{u}) ^ ℵ₀ = ℵ_ 1

def hanf (κ : Cardinal.{u}) : Cardinal.{u} := beth (succ ((2 : Cardinal.{u}) ^ κ)).ord

def endpoint : Cardinal.{u} := beth (ω_ 2)

def tailThreshold : Cardinal.{u} := beth (succ ((2 : Cardinal.{u}) ^ (ℵ_ 1))).ord

theorem main (hCH : CH.{u}) :
    ∃ (L : ℕ → Type), Countable (Σ n, L n) ∧
      ∃ K : ClassData.{u} L, K.IsAEC ∧ K.HasLSNumber ℵ₀ ∧
        endpoint = hanf (ℵ₀ : Cardinal.{u}) ∧ K.TwoModels endpoint ∧
        ∀ μ, tailThreshold ≤ μ → K.Categorical μ := by
  sorry

end CHObstruction

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Assuming the continuum hypothesis, we construct an abstract elementary class with Löwenheim–Skolem number ℵ<sub>0</sub> that is categorical in every sufficiently large cardinal but has at least two nonisomorphic models of cardinality $`\beth_{\omega_2}`$. Thus categoricity does not transfer down to the proposed bound $`\beth_{(2^{\aleph_0})^+}`$, which equals $`\beth_{\omega_2}`$ under CH. Consequently, if ZFC is consistent, the prescribed-threshold form of Shelah's categoricity conjecture is not provable in ZFC.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:hanf-function"></a>
Categoricity asks whether a class has exactly one model of a given cardinality, up to isomorphism. For an abstract elementary class $K$, write $I(K,\kappa)$ for the number of isomorphism types of its models of cardinality $\kappa$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)$ for its Löwenheim–Skolem number. Thus categoricity in $\kappa$ means $I(K,\kappa)=1$. Put $$\label{eq:hanf-function}
 h(\kappa)=\beth_{(2^\kappa)^+},\qquad H(K)=h(\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)).$$ Here cardinal successors are identified with their initial ordinals when used as beth indices, and the beth hierarchy starts at $\beth_0=\aleph_0$. All structures and index sets are set-sized, all languages are finitary, and categoricity includes existence. The AEC axioms are recalled in Section <a href="#sec:aec" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:aec">3</a>.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The prescribed threshold and the result

<a id="eq:target"></a>
For complete first-order theories in a countable language, Morley’s categoricity theorem transfers categoricity from one uncountable cardinal to every uncountable cardinal (Morley 1965) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Morley1965](#ref-Morley1965)]. Abstract elementary classes retain a coherent notion of strong substructure and closure under increasing unions while allowing classes that are not axiomatized by a first-order theory. Shelah introduced this framework in the mid-1970s for classes of models in infinitary logics (Boney and Vasey 2017, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BoneyVasey2017Survey](#ref-BoneyVasey2017Survey)]. His categoricity questions for abstract elementary classes have both qualitative and quantitative forms. Qualitative eventual categoricity asks for a cardinal $\chi(\kappa)$, depending only on the infinite cardinal $\kappa$, such that, whenever $\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)\le\kappa$, categoricity in some $\lambda\ge\chi(\kappa)$ implies categoricity in every $\mu\ge\chi(\kappa)$. The prescribed-threshold form asks for the particular bound in <a href="#eq:hanf-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hanf-function">[eq:hanf-function]</a>: $$\label{eq:target}
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{If \(K\) is categorical in some \(\lambda\ge H(K)\),}\\
 \text{then it is categorical in every \(\mu\ge H(K)\).}
 \end{gathered}$$ No amalgamation, joint embedding, tameness, or absence of maximal models is assumed here. The exact formulation, with both endpoints included, appears in the discussion of the general conjecture in (Šaroch and Trlifaj 2024, sec. 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[SarochTrlifaj2024](#ref-SarochTrlifaj2024)].

The number $H(K)$ is a general existence bound: a model at that size gives arbitrarily large models by the presentation and Ehrenfeucht–Mostowski methods; see (Shelah 1999, Claim 0.6) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Shelah1999CategoricityAP](#ref-Shelah1999CategoricityAP)]. Existence alone does not assert uniqueness or that every given model extends to arbitrarily large ones. Categoricity transfers in the literature obtain stronger conclusions under structural assumptions. For example, amalgamation and $\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)$-tameness yield transfer from a successor categoricity cardinal at least $H(K)$ down to $H(K)$ and throughout the tail (Vasey 2017, Corollary 9.10) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Vasey2017Downward](#ref-Vasey2017Downward)]. The distinction between these assumptions and the bare AEC axioms is essential for the construction below.

The distinction between a prescribed bound and an unspecified uniform threshold is already explicit in Grossberg’s discussion of the two forms of the conjecture (Grossberg 2021, chap. 2, Section 4, Conjectures 4.4–4.6) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Grossberg2021Draft](#ref-Grossberg2021Draft)]. There the possible failure of the prescribed bound is considered separately from eventual categoricity. A transfer at the prescribed bound for arbitrary AECs is claimed in (Espíndola 2023, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Espindola2023Spectra](#ref-Espindola2023Spectra)]. The CH construction below has the opposite conclusion at that bound. Its proof therefore includes explicit verification of all AEC axioms, as well as separate arguments for the endpoint models and for the higher categoricity tail. Section <a href="#sec:booleanization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:booleanization">7</a> also identifies a specific obstruction to the canonical-point step in the cited proof: on the countable-model site, a dense cosieve need not be lifted by a large categorical model. The comparison concerns that specific factorization, not alternative constructions of points.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Assume the continuum hypothesis. There is an abstract elementary class $K$ in a countable finitary relational language such that*

1.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)=\aleph_0$;*

2.  *$K$ has at least two nonisomorphic models of cardinality $\beth_{\omega_2}=H(K)$;*

3.  *putting $\Lambda=\beth_{(2^{\aleph_1})^+}$, the class $K$ is categorical in every cardinal $\mu\ge\Lambda$.*

*In particular, $\lambda=\Lambda^+>H(K)$ is a categoricity cardinal from which downward transfer to $H(K)$ fails.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> refutes the prescribed-threshold transfer under CH. Combining the construction with Gödel’s relative consistency of CH gives the corresponding conditional nonprovability statement; Corollary <a href="#cor:consistency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:consistency">34</a> states it with a precise first-order formulation. The same class is categorical on the higher tail beginning at $\Lambda$, so the obstruction is compatible with qualitative eventual categoricity. The construction and both cardinal bounds are proved here without a qualitative eventual-categoricity theorem.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The mechanism of the construction

There are two principal sorts. The index sort $I$ controls the size of the model. The base sort $B$ is a dense linear order without endpoints whose bounded intervals are countable; hence $|B|\le\aleph_1$. For each nonempty finite ordered tuple of distinct entries from $I$, the structure has row fibers indexed by $\omega$ and column fibers indexed by $B$. A row fiber is a torsor for $[B]^{<\omega}$, and a column fiber for $[\omega]^{<\omega}$, with symmetric difference as addition: choosing an origin identifies a fiber with its group, but no origin is named in the language. Choosing one point in each fiber displays the bit relation as an $\omega$-by-$B$ matrix. Changing row origins alters finitely many entries in each row, and changing column origins alters finitely many entries in each column.

When $B$ is countable, these changes absorb every matrix of bits. All models in $K$ with countable base orders and equally sized index sorts are therefore isomorphic. When $B$ is uncountable, the matrix retains a family of colors in $2^\omega/=^*$, the quotient of binary sequences by equality outside a finite set. These colors are meaningful outside a countable set of columns. We impose tests on them, separately for each tuple. The tests describe countable extensional relations whose rank labels lie below $\omega_1$.

In the following overview, $V_\alpha$ denotes the ordinary cumulative hierarchy of sets. The technical features can be summarized as follows.

1.  *Exceptions compatible with strong substructure.* An object may have countably many exceptional columns for each tuple. A strong inclusion requires every newly added column to pass the tests on old tuples. Finite row supports make these outside tests exactly independent of old choices of origins. This permits both small strong submodels and arbitrary directed unions, including smoothness.

2.  *Local rank approximation.* An $\omega_2$-long sequence of functions $\omega_1\to\omega_1$, increasing modulo countable sets, lets countable membership diagrams drawn from $V_{\omega_2}$ pass the rank tests at all but countably many coordinates. This produces the model of size $\beth_{\omega_2}$ with uncountable $B$.

3.  *An obstruction to large index sets.* A fixed coherent pattern for every finite arity would give arbitrarily large extensional structures with ranks below $\omega_1$, contradicting their collapse into $V_{\omega_1}$. A finite partition argument and an unbounded-sample selection produce such a pattern if $|I|\ge\Lambda$ and $B$ is uncountable.

Finite-support groups, torsors with no named origins, and parity relations have a close predecessor in the Hart–Shelah examples (Hart and Shelah 1990, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HartShelah1990](#ref-HartShelah1990)]. Their canonical structures also use binary-sequence colors modulo finitely supported sequences, $2^\omega/2^{<\omega}$, the same quotient as eventual equality (Hart and Shelah 1990, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HartShelah1990](#ref-HartShelah1990)]. The present construction couples finite row and column translations with a locally countable base order; the surviving colors encode the extensional rank diagrams needed for the endpoint obstruction. Allowed finite color diagrams and ranks also play a central role in the coloring classes studied by Kolesnikov and Lambie-Hanson (Kolesnikov and Lambie-Hanson 2016) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KolesnikovLambieHanson2016](#ref-KolesnikovLambieHanson2016)]. Here the encoded diagrams are countable, and the full-subsequence tests supply the exact compatibility used in their limit.

The first mechanism separates the invariance needed for objects from the exact invariance needed for extensions. The last two mechanisms compare local approximations, with different exceptional sets, to one global pattern. Their proofs are given explicitly; in particular, no infinite homogeneous subset is assumed.

Section <a href="#sec:colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:colors">2</a> defines the diagram tests. Section <a href="#sec:aec" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:aec">3</a> constructs the AEC and verifies all its axioms. Section <a href="#sec:endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:endpoint">4</a> constructs the two endpoint models. Section <a href="#sec:categoricity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:categoricity">5</a> proves the fixed-pattern obstruction, uses it to bound models with uncountable base, and then establishes categoricity on the tail. Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a> combines the results and explains the failure of amalgamation and joint embedding. Section <a href="#sec:booleanization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:booleanization">7</a> proves the dense-cosieve obstruction and makes the precise comparison with the cited topos argument.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Conventions

The construction in Sections <a href="#sec:colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:colors">2</a>–<a href="#sec:categoricity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:categoricity">5</a> is carried out in ZFC with CH. Throughout those sections, <a id="eq:standing-cardinals"></a>$$\label{eq:standing-cardinals}
 \delta=\omega_1,\qquad \eta=\delta^+=\omega_2,\qquad
 S=2^\omega/=^*,\qquad \xi=2^\delta,\qquad
 \Lambda=\beth_{\xi^+}.$$ The relation $=^*$ means equality except at finitely many natural numbers. “Countable” includes finite, and “tuple” in the color tests always means a nonempty finite ordered tuple with distinct entries. Subsequences preserve the order of the remaining positions. The hierarchy $V_\alpha$ is the ordinary cumulative hierarchy of sets; it is unrelated to the model sorts. We use choice for set-indexed families only.

<a id="sec:colors"></a>
### Countable diagrams and their tests

The construction uses colors that encode countable diagrams with ordinal tags. A finite tuple passes a test when its diagram and all subsequence diagrams are valid and agree exactly on their shared labels. These tests will define the object condition in Section <a href="#sec:aec" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:aec">3</a>; the endpoint construction will realize them outside a tuple-dependent countable set of coordinates.

Restrictions and ranks of finite color patterns also occur in coloring classes (Kolesnikov and Lambie-Hanson 2016, Definitions 1.1–1.7) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KolesnikovLambieHanson2016](#ref-KolesnikovLambieHanson2016)]. There the rank measures extensions of finite monochromatic diagrams. Here ordinal tags are assigned to the elements of countable extensional relations, and the tests require exact agreement under restriction.

Throughout the construction we work in ZFC with CH and put $\delta=\omega_1$. For $a,a'\in 2^\omega$, write $a=^*a'$ if $\{n<\omega:a(n)\ne a'(n)\}$ is finite, and set $$S=2^\omega/{=^*}.$$ The quotient here is a set of colors; its elements will later be realized by binary sequences in structures.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Diagrams, codes, and restrictions

For a positive integer $k$, write $[k]=\{1,\ldots,k\}$ and let $$\mathcal L_k
   =\{(J,i):\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k],\ i<\omega\}.$$ The set $\mathcal L_k$ is countably infinite. A label records a nonempty set of tuple positions and an integer coordinate.

<a id="stmt-col-diagram"></a>
<div id="col:diagram" class="definition">

**Definition 2** (Valid diagrams). An *arity-$k$ diagram* is a triple $\mathcal D=(E,R,\rho)$, where $E,R$ are binary relations on $\mathcal L_k$ and $\rho:\mathcal L_k\to\delta$. It is *valid* if the following conditions hold.

1.  $E$ is an equivalence relation. The relation $R$ and the tags respect $E$: whenever $aEa'$ and $bEb'$, $$aRb\ \Longleftrightarrow\ a'Rb',
     \qquad
     \rho(a)=\rho(a').$$

2.  On the quotient $Q_{\mathcal D}=\mathcal L_k/E$, use $R$ and $\rho$ also for the induced relation and tag function. They satisfy $$yRx\ \Longrightarrow\ \rho(y)<\rho(x).$$ The relation $R$ is extensional: distinct $x,x'\in Q_{\mathcal D}$ have different predecessor sets $$\{y\in Q_{\mathcal D}:yRx\}
     \quad\hbox{and}\quad
     \{y\in Q_{\mathcal D}:yRx'\}.$$

3.  The labels $(\{j\},0)$, for $j\in[k]$, lie in pairwise distinct $E$-classes.

</div>

Let $\mathfrak D_k$ be the set of all arity-$k$ diagrams, including the invalid ones.

<a id="stmt-col-coding"></a>
<div id="col:coding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Coding). *There are injections $$\iota_k:\mathfrak D_k\longrightarrow S
                  \qquad(k\ge1)$$ whose ranges are pairwise disjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-col-coding"><summary>Proof of stmt-col-coding</summary>

*Proof.* Each $=^*$-class is countable: its members are obtained by changing the values of one sequence on finitely many integers. If $\kappa=|S|$, choice therefore gives $$2^{\aleph_0}\le\kappa\cdot\aleph_0.$$ The quotient cannot be countable, since then $2^\omega$ would be countable. Infinite cardinal multiplication and the reverse inequality $\kappa\le2^{\aleph_0}$ now give $|S|=2^{\aleph_0}$.

For each $k$, there are at most $2^{\aleph_0}$ choices for each of $E$ and $R$. By CH, $$\delta^{\aleph_0}
   =(2^{\aleph_0})^{\aleph_0}
   =2^{\aleph_0},$$ so there are at most continuum many choices for $\rho$ as well. It follows that $$\left|\coprod_{k\ge1}\mathfrak D_k\right|
       \le\aleph_0\cdot2^{\aleph_0}=|S|.$$ An injection from this disjoint union into $S$ gives the stated maps. ◻

</details>

Fix such coding maps for the remainder of the proof. A color in the range of $\iota_k$ uniquely decodes to an arity-$k$ diagram. A color outside that range is not a code of the required arity. The coding maps are fixed set parameters in the definition of the class, not symbols of its language. Allowing codes for invalid diagrams will let us assign colors at exceptional coordinates.

<a id="col:restriction-composition"></a>
We next specify restriction, including its position reindexing. If $\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]$, enumerate $J=\{j_1<\cdots<j_m\}$ and define $$\ell_J:\mathcal L_m\longrightarrow\mathcal L_k,
 \qquad
 \ell_J(U,i)=(\{j_u:u\in U\},i).$$ For an arity-$k$ diagram $\mathcal D$, its restriction $\mathcal D\restriction J$ is the arity-$m$ diagram obtained by pulling back both relations and the tag function along $\ell_J$. Thus it retains exactly those labels whose position sets are contained in $J$. In particular, for $\varnothing\ne U\subseteq[m]$, $$\label{col:restriction-composition}
 (\mathcal D\restriction J)\restriction U
       =\mathcal D\restriction\{j_u:u\in U\}.$$ Validity of a diagram does not by itself guarantee extensionality of a restriction; the tests below require validity of every constituent diagram separately.

<a id="stmt-col-test"></a>
<div id="col:test" class="definition">

**Definition 4** (The full subsequence test). Given $k\ge1$ and colors $(s_J:\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k])$, let $$T_k\bigl((s_J)_J\bigr)$$ mean that both of the following conditions hold.

1.  For every nonempty $J\subseteq[k]$, the color $s_J$ decodes under $\iota_{|J|}$ to a valid diagram $\mathcal D_J$.

2.  For every nonempty $J\subseteq[k]$, $$\mathcal D_J
                           =\mathcal D_{[k]}\restriction J.$$

For an ordered tuple $t=(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ of distinct elements, write $t\restriction J=(x_{j_1},\ldots,x_{j_m})$. Colors attached to $t$ and its nonempty subsequences pass the test when $T_k((s_{t\restriction J})_J)$ holds.

</div>

The order in this definition is the order of the tuple positions. It requires no order on the set from which tuple entries are chosen. Equation <a href="#col:restriction-composition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="col:restriction-composition">[col:restriction-composition]</a> shows that agreement with the full diagram implies agreement between every two nested subsequences.

<a id="stmt-col-quotients"></a>
<div id="col:quotients" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Maps between quotient diagrams). *Suppose the test in Definition <a href="#col:test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="col:test">4</a> passes. If $\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]$, then the label map $\ell_J$ induces an injective map $$Q_{\mathcal D_J}\longrightarrow
                 Q_{\mathcal D_{[k]}}.$$ This map preserves tags and preserves and reflects $R$. The quotient maps for nested subsequences compose.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-col-quotients"><summary>Proof of stmt-col-quotients</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\mathcal D_J=(E_J,R_J,\rho_J)$. The equality $\mathcal D_J=\mathcal D_{[k]}\restriction J$ gives, for labels $a,b\in\mathcal L_{|J|}$, $$aE_Jb\ \Longleftrightarrow\
       \ell_J(a)E_{[k]}\ell_J(b).$$ The forward implication makes the quotient map well-defined, and the reverse implication makes it injective. The analogous equivalence for $R$ proves preservation and reflection of the quotient relation; equality of the restricted tag functions proves preservation of tags. The maps on labels compose by their definition, so the induced quotient maps do also. ◻

</details>

The tests permit the tuple-dependent diagrams constructed in Section <a href="#sec:endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:endpoint">4</a>. They do not permit one fixed color $s_j$ for each arity $j$ such that $T_k((s_{|J|})_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]})$ holds for every $k\ge1$. Lemma <a href="#col:no-pattern" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="col:no-pattern">25</a> will prove this obstruction; it is the ingredient that later bounds models with uncountable base.

<a id="sec:aec"></a>
### The abstract elementary class

We now turn the tests of Section <a href="#sec:colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:colors">2</a> into a class of structures. The distinction between the object condition and the strong substructure condition is essential: objects allow countably many bad coordinates for each tuple, whereas a strong extension must introduce no bad coordinate for an old tuple outside the old base order.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The axioms and the basic structures

<a id="stmt-aec-definition"></a>
<div id="aec:definition" class="definition">

**Definition 6** (Abstract elementary class). An *abstract elementary class* is a pair $K=(\mathcal C,\le_K)$, where $\mathcal C$ is a class of set-sized structures in one fixed set-sized finitary language $\tau$, and $\le_K$ is a partial order on $\mathcal C$, with the following properties. Write $|M|$ for a structure’s underlying set and $\|M\|$ for its cardinality.

1.  *Isomorphisms and substructures.* The class is closed under $\tau$-isomorphisms; $M\le_K N$ implies that $M$ is a $\tau$-substructure of $N$; and if $f:N\cong N'$ and $M\le_K N$, then $f[M]\le_K N'$.

2.  *Coherence.* If $M_0$ is a $\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and $M_0\le_K M_2$ and $M_1\le_K M_2$, then $M_0\le_K M_1$.

3.  *Unions and smoothness.* For every nonzero ordinal $\gamma$ and every increasing chain $(M_i:i<\gamma)$ under $\le_K$, its union $U$ belongs to $\mathcal C$, and $M_i\le_K U$ for all $i<\gamma$. If also $M_i\le_K N$ for every $i<\gamma$, where $N\in\mathcal C$, then $U\le_K N$.

4.  *Downward Löwenheim–Skolem property.* There is an infinite cardinal $\theta\ge|\tau|+\aleph_0$ such that, for every $M\in\mathcal C$ and every $A\subseteq |M|$, there exists $N\le_K M$ with $A\subseteq |N|$ and $\|N\|\le |A|+\theta$.

The least such infinite cardinal $\theta$ is denoted by $\mathop{\mathrm{LS}}(K)$.

</div>

We will prove the union and common-upper-bound assertions directly for every nonempty set-indexed directed family of strong substructures. This in particular proves all the chain assertions in Definition <a href="#aec:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:definition">6</a>, with no restriction on the chain’s cofinality.

For a nonempty set $I$, let $\mathcal T(I)$ be the set of all nonempty finite ordered tuples of distinct members of $I$. If $t=(a_1,\ldots,a_k)$ and $\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]$, write $t\restriction J$ for the subsequence in the original order. No order is placed on the sort $I$.

Finite-support groups and torsors also occur in the Hart–Shelah construction (Hart and Shelah 1990, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HartShelah1990](#ref-HartShelah1990)]; see also (Boney and Vasey 2018, Definition 3.1 and Fact 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BoneyVasey2018HartShelah](#ref-BoneyVasey2018HartShelah)]. We specify two families of torsors, one for rows and one for columns, and the precise finite-support action on their common bit relation.

<a id="stmt-aec-basic"></a>
<div id="aec:basic" class="definition">

**Definition 7** (Basic structures). A basic structure has the following data, with pairwise disjoint underlying carriers.

1.  A nonempty set $I$, and a nonempty dense linear order $B$ without endpoints in which every bounded interval is countable.

2.  A group sort $G(B)$ coding $[B]^{<\omega}$ under symmetric difference. More precisely, an incidence relation determines a bijection $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}:G(B)\longrightarrow[B]^{<\omega},
     \qquad
     \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u+u')=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u)\triangle\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u').$$ There is also a group sort $G_*$ identified, by a separate unary singleton label for each of its elements, with $[\omega]^{<\omega}$ under symmetric difference. These labels exhaust $G_*$.

3.  For each $t\in\mathcal T(I)$ and $n<\omega$, a nonempty set $Z_{t,n}$ with a free transitive action of $G(B)$. For each $t\in\mathcal T(I)$ and $b\in B$, a nonempty set $W_{t,b}$ with a free transitive action of $G_*$. We write both actions additively. Thus each element of a fiber is obtained uniquely by translating any one chosen element of that fiber.

<a id="aec:bit"></a>
4.  A binary relation on row and column points, written as its indicator $e(z,w)\in\{0,1\}$. It can hold only when the row and column have the same tuple $t$. On these pairs it satisfies $$\label{aec:bit}
     e(z+u,w+v)
     =
     e(z,w)+\boldsymbol 1_{b\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u)}
           +\boldsymbol 1_{n\in v}\pmod 2$$ for $z\in Z_{t,n}$, $w\in W_{t,b}$, $u\in G(B)$, and $v\in G_*$. In the last term $v$ is read through its fixed finite-subset label. Here $\boldsymbol 1_P$ is $1$ when $P$ holds and $0$ otherwise.

The domain consists exactly of the listed carriers. There are no additional relations or distinguished representatives.

</div>

Here is an explicit countable finitary relational language for these data. Use unary predicates for $I,B,G(B),G_*$, for each carrier $$Z_{k,n}=\bigcup_{\substack{t\in\mathcal T(I)\\|t|=k}}Z_{t,n}
 \quad(k\ge1,\ n<\omega),
 \qquad
 W_k=\bigcup_{\substack{t\in\mathcal T(I)\\|t|=k,\ b\in B}}W_{t,b},$$ and for each singleton label in $G_*$. The carrier predicates, excluding the labels within $G_*$, partition the domain. For each $k,n$, a $(k+1)$-ary projection graph associates a row point with its $k$ tuple entries. For each $k$, a $(k+2)$-ary projection graph associates a column point with its tuple entries and its coordinate $b$. These projections are semantically total and single-valued on their carriers, have distinct tuple coordinates in $I$, and have exactly the nonempty fibers required in Definition <a href="#aec:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:basic">7</a>. A carrier may be empty if there are no tuples of its length.

Use binary relations for the order, finite-set incidence, and the bit relation, and ternary graphs for group operations and the two actions. Every operation and action is semantically total on its stated domain. Operation graphs give precisely the specified groups; action graphs preserve the indicated fibers and give precisely the free transitive actions. There are no relation instances outside these specified types. In particular, bits between different tuple fibers are zero. This last requirement rules out any unspecified structure linking different tuples.

All the symbols just listed are indexed by countable sets, and each has finite arity. The language is therefore countable and finitary. Using operation graphs is harmless: in an inclusion of induced relational substructures, a graph triple already in the smaller object remains in the larger one. Semantic uniqueness in the larger object forces it to give the same operation or action value on the old arguments. The smaller object’s semantic totality supplies the needed closure on those arguments.

The fixed coding maps and tests from Section <a href="#sec:colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:colors">2</a> are set parameters in the definition of the class, not additional language symbols. Neither the countability restrictions nor the condition using these tests needs to be first-order expressible.

<a id="stmt-aec-basic-inclusion"></a>
<div id="aec:basic-inclusion" class="definition">

**Definition 8** (Basic inclusion). For basic structures $M_0,M_1$, write $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$ if $M_0$ is an induced $\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and the following further conditions hold:

1.  $G_*^{M_0}=G_*^{M_1}$ as underlying sets;

2.  $B^{M_0}$ is a convex suborder of $B^{M_1}$;

3.  for every $u\in G(B)^{M_0}$, $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}^{M_1}(u)=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}^{M_0}(u)$.

All inclusions here and below allow equality.

</div>

Condition (iii) forbids an old finite-set code from acquiring new members in the larger base order. Since support gives a bijection onto finite subsets in both objects, this condition makes the old group exactly the naturally included copy of $[B^{M_0}]^{<\omega}$ in $[B^{M_1}]^{<\omega}$. The group elements themselves may have arbitrary underlying codes. Basic inclusions compose: induced substructures, convexity, equality of $G_*$, and support preservation each compose.

<a id="stmt-aec-order-size"></a>
<div id="aec:order-size" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Size of the base order). *Every order $B$ in a basic structure has cardinality at most $\aleph_1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aec-order-size"><summary>Proof of stmt-aec-order-size</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $p\in B$. If the final segment above $p$ has a countable cofinal subset, its cardinality is countable: it is covered by countably many bounded intervals with one endpoint $p$, together with their endpoints. Otherwise one can recursively choose a strictly increasing sequence $(b_\alpha:\alpha<\omega_1)$ above $p$. At each stage there are only countably many earlier choices, so the assumption that no countable set is cofinal provides a point above all of them. This sequence must be cofinal above $p$. A point above the whole sequence would enclose uncountably many $b_\alpha$’s in a bounded interval, contrary to local countability. The final segment is therefore covered by at most $\aleph_1$ countable bounded intervals and their endpoints. It has size at most $\aleph_1$. The same argument in the reversed order applies below $p$, proving the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-aec-size"></a>
<div id="aec:size" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Size of a basic structure). *For every basic structure $M$, <a id="aec:size-equation"></a>$$\label{aec:size-equation}
 \|M\|=\max\bigl(|I^M|,|B^M|,\aleph_0\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aec-size"><summary>Proof of stmt-aec-size</summary>

*Proof.* The right-hand side is a lower bound, since $I,B,G_*$ are sorts and $G_*$ is countably infinite. For the upper bound put $\kappa=\max(|I|,|B|,\aleph_0)$. There are at most $\kappa$ finite injective tuples over $I$. The order $B$ is infinite, so $|G(B)|=|B|$; hence every row fiber has size $|B|$, and every column fiber has size $|G_*|=\aleph_0$. There are at most $\kappa$ row fibers, indexed by $\mathcal T(I)\times\omega$, and at most $\kappa$ column fibers, indexed by $\mathcal T(I)\times B$. All the carriers together consequently have size at most $\kappa$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Colors, objects, and strong inclusions

<a id="aec:color"></a>
A *row-choice system* in a basic structure is a family $\mathbf z=(z_{t,n}:t\in\mathcal T(I),\,n<\omega)$ with $z_{t,n}\in Z_{t,n}$. Such systems exist by choice, since the structure and the indexing set are set-sized. For $b\in B$ define $$\label{aec:color}
 c_t^{\mathbf z}(b)
 =
 \bigl[(e(z_{t,n},w))_{n<\omega}\bigr]_{=^*}\in S,
 \qquad w\in W_{t,b}.$$

<a id="stmt-aec-representatives"></a>
<div id="aec:representatives" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Representative independence). *The color in <a href="#aec:color" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aec:color">[aec:color]</a> is independent of $w$. If $\mathbf z,\mathbf z'$ are two row-choice systems, then, for each fixed $t$, their colors $c_t^{\mathbf z}(b)$ and $c_t^{\mathbf z'}(b)$ agree outside a countable subset of $B$. The full subsequence test for a fixed tuple therefore has the same truth value for the two systems outside a countable set.*

*More strongly, suppose $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$. For a tuple in $\mathcal T(I^{M_0})$ and a point $b\in B^{M_1}\setminus B^{M_0}$, compute colors in $M_1$ using row representatives from $M_0$. These colors are independent of every such row choice, with no exceptional coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aec-representatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-aec-representatives</summary>

*Proof.* Two column representatives differ by a translator $v\in G_*$. Equation <a href="#aec:bit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aec:bit">[aec:bit]</a> changes their binary sequences only at the finitely many indices in $v$, so their classes modulo $=^*$ agree.

For a fixed tuple $t$, write $z'_{t,n}=z_{t,n}+u_{t,n}$, with $u_{t,n}\in G(B)$. Put $$D_t=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(u_{t,n}).$$ Each support is finite, so $D_t$ is countable. At every $b\notin D_t$, all coordinates of the two bit sequences are identical when computed with the same column representative. The full test for a tuple of length $k$ involves just its $2^k-1$ nonempty subsequences. Outside the union of their sets $D_{t\restriction J}$, all its input colors agree, proving the test assertion.

In the last assertion, the translators between two old representatives belong to $G(B)^{M_0}$. Their supports in $M_1$ are still subsets of $B^{M_0}$, by basic inclusion. At any $b$ outside $B^{M_0}$, Equation <a href="#aec:bit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aec:bit">[aec:bit]</a> therefore leaves every individual bit unchanged. This proves the stronger assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="aec:bad-set"></a>
For a row-choice system $\mathbf z$ and a tuple $t=(a_1,\ldots,a_k)$, let $$\label{aec:bad-set}
 E_t(\mathbf z)=
 \left\{b\in B:
 \neg T_k\bigl((c_{t\restriction J}^{\mathbf z}(b))_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq[k]}\bigr)
 \right\},$$ where $T_k$ is the full subsequence test of Definition <a href="#col:test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="col:test">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-aec-objects"></a>
<div id="aec:objects" class="definition">

**Definition 12** (Objects and strong substructures). A basic structure is an *object* if $E_t(\mathbf z)$ is countable for every $t\in\mathcal T(I)$, for one, equivalently every, row-choice system $\mathbf z$. Let $\mathcal C$ be the class of objects.

For $M_0,M_1\in\mathcal C$, declare $M_0\le_K M_1$ if $M_0\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm b}M_1$, and, for every $t\in\mathcal T(I^{M_0})$ and every $b\in B^{M_1}\setminus B^{M_0}$, the full test for $t$ passes when its colors in $M_1$ are computed using row representatives in $M_0$, for $t$ and all its nonempty subsequences. Put $K=(\mathcal C,\le_K)$.

</div>

Both formulations are well-defined by Lemma <a href="#aec:representatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:representatives">11</a>. The exceptional set in the object condition is allowed to depend on $t$; no single countable set is required to work for all tuples. In a strong inclusion, there are no exceptions among the new base-order points.

<a id="stmt-aec-arrays"></a>
<div id="aec:arrays" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Free realization of bit arrays). *Let $I\ne\varnothing$, and let $B$ be an order satisfying Definition <a href="#aec:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="aec:basic">7</a>(a). For every family of binary sequences $(a_{t,b}\in 2^\omega:t\in\mathcal T(I),\,b\in B)$, there is a basic structure whose index and base sorts are disjoint copies of $I$ and $B$, with row-choice colors, under these identifications, $$c_t(b)=[a_{t,b}]_{=^*}.$$ If $B$ is countable, every basic structure with base sort $B$ is an object.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aec-arrays"><summary>Proof of stmt-aec-arrays</summary>

*Proof.* Replace $I$ and $B$ by disjoint copies and transport the array indices along these identifications. Use separate disjoint copies for the two group sorts as well. Take tagged, disjoint copies of the appropriate groups for all the fibers. Denote the row points by $(t,n,u)$ for $u\in[B]^{<\omega}$, and the column points by $(t,b,v)$ for $v\in[\omega]^{<\omega}$; the tags distinguish the carrier types. Use the regular translation actions and put <a id="aec:array-realization"></a>$$\label{aec:array-realization}
 e((t,n,u),(t,b,v))
 =
 a_{t,b}(n)+\boldsymbol 1_{b\in u}
           +\boldsymbol 1_{n\in v}\pmod2.$$ Set bits between different tuples to zero, and equip the carriers with exactly the specified projections and other relations. Translation by symmetric difference gives <a href="#aec:bit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="aec:bit">[aec:bit]</a>. The zero row representatives and a zero column representative give the sequence $a_{t,b}$, as required. Finally, when $B$ is countable, each set $E_t(\mathbf z)\subseteq B$ is countable automatically. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Isomorphisms, the partial order, and coherence

<a id="stmt-aec-order-coherence"></a>
<div id="aec:order-coherence" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *The class and strong relation are isomorphism invariant, $\le_K$ is a partial order whose comparisons are induced substructure inclusions, and coherence holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-aec-order-coherence"><summary>Proof of stmt-aec-order-coherence</summary>

*Proof.* All the basic conditions are preserved by a $\tau$-isomorphism, so the isomorphic image of a basic structure is basic. Now consider an isomorphism of basic structures. It preserves the carrier predicates, every row number $n$, all tuple-coordinate projections, the fixed labels of $G_*$, the order, supports, actions, and bits. Transporting a row-choice system along it thus preserves the color at each corresponding base point. Countable bad sets are transported to countable bad sets. This proves invariance of the object condition. Convexity, fixed $G_*$, and equality of old supports are likewise preserved under an isomorphism of a pair of structures. Applying the same color calculation at each new base point proves invariance of the strong relation. In particular, if $f:N\cong N'$ and $M\le_K N$, then $f[M]\le_K N'$.

Reflexivity follows because equality is a basic inclusion and the additional condition has an empty set of new base points. Antisymmetry follows from induced substructure inclusion. To prove transitivity, suppose $M_0\le_K M_1\le_K M_2$. Basic inclusions compose. Fix a tuple $t$ from $I^{M_0}$, a point $b\in B^{M_2}\setminus B^{M_0}$, and row representatives in $M_0$ for $t$ and its nonempty subsequences. If $b\in B^{M_1}\setminus B^{M_0}$, choose its column representatives in $M_1$. The bits against these old rows are unchanged in $M_2$, by induced substructure, and the tests hold by $M_0\le_K M_1$. If $b\notin B^{M_1}$, the selected rows in $M_0$ are also allowable old representatives for $M_1\le_K M_2$; this latter inclusion supplies the tests. Hence $M_0\le_K M_2$.

For coherence, suppose that $M_0$ is an induced $\tau$-substructure of $M_1$, and that both are strong substructures of $M_2$. Their $G_*$ sorts both equal that of $M_2$.
